Improved intelligent decision model for electric vehicle battery using q-Rung orthopair fuzzy Schweizer–Sklar prioritized Z-information
Authors: Abid Ali, Hisam Uddin Shaikh, Shahzaib Ashraf, Ayele Tulu
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The growing interest in the electric vehicles (EVs) has highlighted the significance of choosing appropriate battery technologies according to various competing and unpredictable norms. In the development of EV the selection of appropriate battery is a crucial step, as this component directly affects the cost, performance and ecological system. This task often includes technical, economic and ecological system which are directly incorporated with uncertainty, ambiguity and imprecision. In order to enhance the decision making process of these complex environments, the current study presents a new category of Schweizer-Sklar based prioritized aggregation operators under q-Rung Orthopair Fuzzy Z-Numbers (qROFZNs) framework. These operators are particularly constructed to have prioritization between the attributes and are well prepared to address the increased levels of uncertainty in expert judgments. In order to confirm the efficacy of the suggested strategy, the overall case study of EV battery options is carried out. The findings indicate the way the designed operators increase the ranking stability, reliability, and flexibility in the selection. The results reveal that the suggested operators outperform existing fuzzy MCDM approaches in terms of stability and reliability, as well as robustness to parameter fluctuations. In the name of comparative analysis, the WASPAS model is implemented in the qROFZ context with the first time and some characteristic and analytical comparisons are made between the given model and the available fuzzy MCDM methods. These reviews prove that the developed approach is able to guarantee not only the consistency of the obtained decision but also outcompete the conventional models in regard to their reliability, strength, and flexibility. The results indicate that the proposed framework is a more potent and versatile instrument to use by decision-makers, who have to address uncertainty, and it can be easily extended to other complicated sustainability-related decision-making issues, including EV battery selection.

Introduction

Electric cars, or EVs, have emerged as a crucial instrument in the fight against climate change, the reduction of dependency on fossil fuels, and the advancement of ecologically friendly transportation. The battery system is fundamental to any electric vehicle, influencing its price, performance, range, and environmental impact1. However, choosing the right electric battery can be difficult because there are numerous factors (sometimes contradicting) to consider, such as energy density, charge time, cost, endurance, durability, and ecological sustain-ability. All of this makes the difficulties and uncertainty involved with selecting the right battery option even more challenging. In this context, there are Multi-Criteria Decision-Making (MCDM) processes that are relevant and significant because they allow for organized and systematic approaches to evaluating and prioritizing the options. Classical MCDM methods are applicable where the data available is accurate and definite but they fail to deliver meaningful results where there is uncertainty, vagueness and ambiguity in the data at hand. The fuzzy set (FS) theory provides base to MCDM by allowing uncertainty, imprecision, partial truth and hesitation in evaluation for solving real world problems. Using fuzzy extensions in MCDM problems, stake holders can combine quantitative and qualitative components, evaluate their significance, and ultimately select the ideal battery option that satisfies technical requirements, economic feasibility, and sustain-ability requirements. There are many other interlink EVs MCDM problem like charging station allocation2,3, scheduling of battery swapping stations4,5, charging station based on solar energy6 and how maintain traffic congestion in rush hours7.

Literature review

Classical mathematical modeling can help with decision-making, but it is often insufficient in areas such as artificial intelligence, machine learning, financial risk management, and quickly developing technology. In traditional set theory, problems are represented by binary values: 0 denotes that an element does not belong to a set, and 1 shows full membership. However, many real-world problems contain ambiguity and imprecision, which cannot be represented by such clear boundaries. In 1961, Zadeh8 introduced FS theory which overcomes this issue by allowing elements to have partial membership values in the [0,1] range, which captures the slow transitions and ambiguity inherent in human thinking and complicated decision-making situations. The FS theory enhances decision-making and facilitates effective supply chain coordination by managing supply and demand uncertainty. For example, a powerful method for managing uncertainty and multiple aspects is the fuzzy AHP and fuzzy MOORA strategy when selecting the appropriate industrial engineering sector9. In engineering and technology evaluation, fuzzy MCDM methods such as fuzzy MOORA 10 in ranking alternatives in the face of uncertainty, fuzzy AHP 11 in weighting criteria in complex trade-offs and hesitant fuzzy distance 12 measures in comparisons of solutions when decision information is unavailable have all been applied. Hence, FSs have been used to solve various decision-making problems in science, engineering, and management, and they continue to serve as the foundation for the creation of more powerful fuzzy extensions.

In 1983, Atanassov13 made an extension by introducing degree of non-membership function in FSs called Intuitionistic fuzzy set (IFSs). This additional degree of freedom, also known as the hesitation margin, does not reflect uncertainty or indeterminacy in traditional FSs, where membership and non-membership are strictly complementary. Here, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mu } (\mathfrak {x})$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\nu }(\mathfrak {x})$$\end{document} be the degree of membership (MD) and nonmembership degree (ND) of any arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {x} \in \mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} respectively then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \widehat{\mu }(\mathfrak {x}) + \widehat{\nu }(\mathfrak {x}) \le 1$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mu }(\mathfrak {x}), \widehat{\nu }(\mathfrak {x}) \in [0, 1]$$\end{document} and the degree of hesitation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=1-\widehat{\mu }(\mathfrak {x})-\widehat{\nu }(\mathfrak {x})$$\end{document}. The framework of Atanassov was formulated under the necessity to define the situation when it is hard to define MD only due to ambiguous or insufficient information, e.g. in uncertain decision-making or due to false human judgements. In situations where the state of an element cannot be expressed satisfactorily either in full MD or complete exclusion, IFSs offer a more precise way to deal with imprecision in mathematical modeling by formalizing this indeterminacy and permit a more subtle account of real-world situations14. Recently, the IFS was utilized in the solution to decision-support problems using the hesitant fuzzy distance in combination15. 1 The IFSs is made to work on any uncertain, ambiguous and imprecision data which makes it a strong extension of classical FSs.

Yager extended this in 2013 by further allowing more flexible and adaptive modeling with MD and ND and named it as Pythagorean fuzzy set (PyFSs) where sum of the square of MD and ND is less than or equal to one i.e, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \widehat{\mu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x}) + \widehat{\nu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x}) \le 1$$\end{document}, here degree of hesitation is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =\sqrt{1-\widehat{\mu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x})-\widehat{\nu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x})}$$\end{document}. In 2014, Zhang and Xu17 used PyFSs in MCDM by extension of TOPSIS to tackle uncertain, ambiguous and complex data by expanding the rang of adoptability. Yager18 proved that PyFSs is more efficient and effective in incomplete and uncertain information by offering tremendous mathematical modeling to cover whole scenario. The application of PyFSs are widely used in many diverse field, PyFSs worked as leverage in handling imprecise, uncertain and ambiguous information. They are frequently employed in MCDM to rank options in uncertain and ambiguous circumstances, as several works have examined19,20. PyFSs are effective at enhancing image processing jobs such as edge detection and segmentation by handling the information of ambiguous pixels21. They assist in the process of supplier selection in supply chain management by managing the uncertainties in evaluation criteria used in supplier selection process22,23, and other fields.

Later on, Yager proposed q-rung orthopair fuzzy sets (qROFSs) as a very adaptable framework for uncertainty modeling. Both IFSs and PyFSs serve as their foundation. The PyFS minimize the limitation of IFS as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mu }(\mathfrak {x}) + \widehat{\nu }(\mathfrak {x}) > 1$$\end{document} in same pattern the qROFS overcome the limitation of PyFS as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x}) + \widehat{\nu }^{2}(\mathfrak {x}) > 1$$\end{document}. The qROFSs is generalized form of IFS and PyFS by permit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu ^q + \mu ^q \le 1$$\end{document} for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \ge 1$$\end{document}, where degree of hesitation defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi = \left( 1 - \widehat{\mu }^{q}(\mathfrak {x}) - \widehat{\nu }^{q}(\mathfrak {x})\right) ^{1/q}$$\end{document}24. Building on Yagers 2013 work with PyFSs, this approach introduces significantly greater flexibility in modeling complex forms of uncertainty, positioning qROFSs as a valuable tool for managing ambiguous datasets in analytical and decision-support contexts25–28, AI and big data analytics29. Modern research is investigating how they may be utilized in bigger decision support frameworks, demonstrating that they are still in development and have many potential applications.

In 2011, Zadeh proposed Z-numbers (30), which considerably enhanced our understanding of confusing information. A Z-number is formally recognized as an ordered pair, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z = (A, B)$$\end{document}, where A is a fuzzy number describing the unknown variable and B is another fuzzy number reflecting the level of dependability or credibility associated with A. This two-tiered structure provides a more complex way to characterize uncertainty than classic fuzzy sets, which typically express inconsistency merely through membership degrees (MD). Zadeh’s work broadens the scope of fuzzy frameworks, allowing for not only the description of uncertainty but also the explicit measurement of confidence in the information supplied. This criterion enables Z-numbers to reflect both data errors and doubt about its reliability, providing a broader approach in taking action in the face of complicated ambiguity. The notion expands on Zadeh’s previous work31 by employing fuzzy logic to real-world circumstances with confusing and partially accurate information. Z-numbers are extremely useful for applications that need substantial uncertainties in modeling, such as risk assessment and expert systems32. Z-numbers are used in domains such as optimized supply chains and healthcare evaluation to improve option selection by integrating reliability. Furthermore, Z-numbers aid in linguistic decision-making by imitating human-like reasoning in tasks such as evaluation of projects and resource allocation.

In 2024, Kumar and Gupta33 integrate the q-rung orthopair fuzzy set with Z-number and developed a novel set called q-rung orthopair Z-number (qROFZN) in FS theory to make TOPSIS algorithm which helped in decision making process. Here they used \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu , \mu$$\end{document} as MD and ND respectively, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z(\nu ),Z(\mu ))$$\end{document} their reliability respectively such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z(\nu ))^q+(Z(\mu ))^q \le 1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \ge 1$$\end{document}. The study sets a novel idea of expressing uncertainty with reliability measure. But up till now, no more work has been done over this set. So, this study now proposed aggregation operations and discusses other MCDM techniques over this structure. In real world scenario like selection of EVs battery the IFS does not captured the reliability of data specifically when expert opinion on cycle life, charging time and safety etc. the data credibility is neglected. Further the restriction, sum of MD and ND less than one unable IFS to evaluate higher degree of uncertainty. These restrictions of reliability of data and degree of higher order uncertainty derive us to elaborate Z-number and qROFSs to cover reliability and higher order uncertainty.

Motivation of the study

Although traditional MCDM methods can offer a structured analysis of multiple criteria, it does not allow addressing the uncertainty, vagueness and reliability issues that are involved in EV battery selection. The IFS and PyFS approaches unable to evaluate higher order uncertainty and the reliability of data is also compromised these limitation lead us the possibilities to extend the integration of qROFSs with Z-numbers to provide the possibility of enhanced modeling for higher order uncertainty and reliability of data will be an interesting direction in developing next-generation MCDM solutions. The fact that the q-Rung Orthopair Fuzzy Z-Number (qROFZN) framework considers both membership and non-membership data and, at the same time, establishes the reliability of expert evaluation based on the Z-number element is an important benefit. Additionally, the q-rung structure increases the expressive power of values, allowing decision-makers to express preferences in a more flexible way compared to IFSs and PyFSs.

The conventional MCDM models have shown that the weighted averaging, ordered, or Dombi aggregation operators are restricted to balance among conflicting criteria due to imposing a predetermined form of trade-off. As an example, whereas weighted averaging can be used to have proportional effect of criteria, it cannot be used to reflect extreme conjunctive or disjunctive cases without redefining weights. This inflexibility is undesirable in battery selection in EVs, where the criteria include cost, energy density, charging time, safety, and environmental impact are not only conflicting but also situational. Schweizer–Sklar aggregation provides such a deficiency by adding a scalar parameter which can be tuned by decision-makers to alternatively represent conservative (risk-averse, conjunctive) and optimistic (risk-tolerant, disjunctive) strategies in the same operator. Flexible aggregation operators are provided by the Schweizer–Sklar approach for handling unclear and ambiguous data in MCDM issues. It began to be developed in the 1960 as a result of the work of Schweizer and Sklar on t-norms and t-conorms34. Its parametric method improves the decision-making of complex situations, including the selection of suppliers35, by allowing the aggregation strictness to be changed36. It can be effectively used to deal with uncertainty and the relationship among the criteria because of fuzzy sets and prioritized aggregation combination operation as well as the management of both inter-criteria relationship and uncertainty is performed effectively and efficiently by the methodology of fuzzy sets and prioritized aggregation operation37. This flexibility is essential to sound MCDM applications in disciplines like engineering and sustain-ability.

Thus, the SS aggregation would be a more subtle and context-specific solution that, when added to prioritized and weighted-prioritized qROFZNs, would make the decision-making process more reliable and flexible. Thus these limitation motivate us to use the Schweizer–Sklar aggregation operators has tunable parameter that manage trade-off between conjunctive and disjunctive as applied in this context is suitable because the concept is customizable and mathematically consistent in balancing conflicting criteria within a complex decision environment. On this basis, the prioritized and weighted-prioritized aggregation operators of Schweizer–Sklar are further additions to the decision process which enable the systematic consideration of the level of expert priorities in the criteria so that more decisive attributes have a stronger effect on the ultimate ranking.

Moreover, the selection of EVs battery criteria includes cycle life, charging time, energy density, safety, and ecological effect involves inherent uncertainty and conflicting trade-offs which motivate us to use fuzzy model for the selection of EVs battery. More advanced aggregation systems are urgently required, which not only define criteria priorities but also evaluate the reliability of the information in ways that classic fuzzy systems do not. Overall, these characteristics make the proposed method more structured, efficient, and well-suited of dealing with the intricate EV battery selection trade-offs than classic fuzzy and crisp MCDM approaches.

Listed below outline a few of the reasons that motivate us to work on it:The Schweizer-Sklar aggregation operator, which includes the t-norm and t-conorm, is a flexible and generalized technique for aggregating fuzzy information, allowing for specific modeling as necessary in MCDM scenarios.The q-rung orthopair fuzzy sets (qROFS) and Z-numbers are an effective paradigm for dealing with growing degrees of uncertainty, vagueness, and data unreliability in dimensional MCDM models.The ranking methods such as TOPSIS, VIKOR or PROMETHE are distance based evaluation method and the weighted sum model and weighted product models are incorporated in the WASPAS approach, which makes the weighted sum model and weighted product models simple yet a powerful tool in ranking the data without aggregation in MCDM. This stimulates the combination of WASPAS and qROFZNs to receive more realistic and practical ranking to the final outcome.An effective, efficient and novel decision making support tool that integrate qROFZN with Schweizer–Sklar aggregation and WASPAS separately can be developed to increase the flexibility, adaptability, accuracy and realistic solutions in the processing of MCDM.The above motivational points drive us to make combination of Schweizer–Sklar t-norm and t-conorm with qROFZN to develop aggregation operators in which prioritized weight are used. This combination will lead to minimize uncertainty, ambiguity and imprecision of data in selection of EVs battery. Research on these collective methods will directly solve the challenges involve in EV battery selection, ensuring that the proposed fuzzy decision model is not only accurate but also flexible and adaptable to real-world trade-offs.

Objectives of the study

Following are core objectives which will be achieved during the research:The main goal of this research is to a thorough analysis of effectiveness and efficiency of Schweizer–Sklar norms combining with qROFZN in MCDM process to attain optimum solution.In the presented research, define the fundamental operations of Schweize–Skalr T-norm and T-conorm in the context of qROFZN. These operations address the limitations of earlier models by employing adaptable, context-sensitive inference approaches to enhance MCDM process.Apply the Schweizer–Sklar prioritize aggregation operators in context of qROFZN on MCDM problem to select the batteries for electric vehicles to check the efficiency and effectiveness of proposed work.Moreover, make a comparative analysis of Schweizer–Sklar aggregation operators with WASPAS method that make the proposed method solid, authentic and efficient.The aim of the research is to provide a novel approach of Schweizer–Sklar aggregation operator fuse qROFZN overcome the limitations of imprecision and reliability in efficient and effective manners. The combination of q- rung orthopair fuzzy Z-number and Schweizer-Sklar prioritize aggregation operators provide a strong and novel MCDM-framework that fills two gaps simultaneously in imprecision and uncertainty based decision making. Beyond the competencies of IFS and PyFS this combination tackle the reliability of data by using Z-number and wide range of expert hesitancy with help of parameter q. By development of Schweizer Sklar prioritize aggregation operators with q-rung orthopair fuzzy Z-number introduced flexibility and adaptability tuning of aggregation process with parameter r to tackle optimistic and pessimistic risk preferences without any change in operational process. The proposed method extends several existing aggregation models like FS, IFS, PyFS, qROFS, IFZN, PyFZN etc. as special cases, thereby enhancing flexibility, robustness, and interpretability in uncertain MCDM problems such as EV battery selection.

Structure of the study

The structure of the research as follows: Section 2 provide the preliminaries i.e, basic definitions of qROFZN, Schweizer Sklar norms and their operations. Section 3, explain the definitions and proof of the Schweizer Sklar aggregation operators in context of qROFZN and vise versa. Section 4 provides the algorithm to solve MCDM problems by using the qROFZN in context of Schweizer Sklar method. Section 5 conduct case study and solves the numerical example for selection of best electric vehicle battery among available options while the section 6 presents the WASPAS approach over qROFZN and solve a numerical example. Section 7 gives the comparative analysis of proposed method with existing methodologies, proposed WASPAS approach. This section also discusses Sensitivity Analysis, practical implication. The section 8 provides the conclusion of whole research, discuss limitations and gives some suggestions for further research.

Preliminaries

This section gives an overview of basic definitions and fundamental operations that provide help in proposed research work.

Definition 2.3

16 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} be a universal set then pythagorean fuzzy set is define as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \breve{\zeta }=\{(\mathfrak {x},(\phi (\mathfrak {x}),\tau (\mathfrak {x})))|\mathfrak {x}\in \mathfrak {X}\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi : \mathfrak {X}\longrightarrow [0, 1]$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau : \mathfrak {X}\longrightarrow [0, 1]$$\end{document} are the degree of membership and non-membership grade respectively with the condition: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le (\phi (\mathfrak {x}))^{2}+(\tau (\mathfrak {x}))^{2}\le 1$$\end{document}. The value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (\mathfrak {x}) = \sqrt{1 - \phi (\mathfrak {x})^2 - \tau (\mathfrak {x})^2}$$\end{document}, called the hesitancy degree, quantifies the uncertainty associated with the membership of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {x}$$\end{document}.

Definition 2.4

24 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} be a universal set then q-rung orthopair fuzzy set is define as:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \breve{\zeta }=\{(\mathfrak {x},(\phi (\mathfrak {x}),\tau (\mathfrak {x})))|\mathfrak {x}\in \mathfrak {X}\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi : \mathfrak {X}\longrightarrow [0, 1]$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau : \mathfrak {X}\longrightarrow [0, 1]$$\end{document} are the degree of membership and non-membership grade respectively with the condition: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le (\phi (\mathfrak {x}))^{q}+(\tau (\mathfrak {x}))^{q}\le 1, \forall \ q\ge 1$$\end{document}.

Definition 2.5

30 A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{Z}$$\end{document} number is defined as an ordered pair of fuzzy number incorporated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z=(L, M)$$\end{document} where L is degree of membership grade and M is the reliability of L.

Definition 2.9

Definition 2.10

34 The Schweizer-Sklar T-Norm and T-Conorm are respectively defined as:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & T_r(\alpha , \beta )=1-(\alpha +\beta -1)^{\frac{1}{r}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & T^*_r(\alpha , \beta )=((1-\alpha )^r+(1-\beta )^r-1)^{\frac{1}{r}},~\text {where}~r<0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proposed aggregation operators

In this section we define novel aggregation operators, this framework combine the qROFZNs with Schweizer-sklar aggregation operators. This combination provide more flexible modeling to solve MCDM problem than existing models in complex and ambiguous environment.

Schweizer-Sklar based basic operations on qROFZNs

In this section we discussed Schweizer-Sklar priorities aggregation operators and notions with some basic operators.

Definition 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_1}=\{(\phi _{L_1}, \phi _{M_1}),(\eta _{L_1}, \eta _{M_1})\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_2}=\{(\phi _{L_2}, \phi _{M_2}),(\eta _{L_2}, \eta _{M_2})\}$$\end{document} be two qROFZN then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (1)~\daleth _{Q_1}\bigoplus \daleth _{Q_2}= & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} \left( \begin{array}{c}\root q \of {1-((1-\phi _{L_1}^q)^r+(1-\phi _{L_2}^q)^r-1~)^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \\ \root q \of {1-((1-\phi _{M_1}^q)^r+(1-\phi _{M_2}^q)^r-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}} \end{array}\right) , \\ \left( \root q \of {((\eta _{L_1})^{qr}+(\eta _{L_2})^{qr}-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}},\root q \of {((\eta _{M_1})^{qr}+ (\eta _{M_2})^{qr}-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}}~\right) \end{array} \right\} .\\ (2)~\daleth _{Q_1}\bigotimes \daleth _{Q_1}= & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} \left( \root q \of {((\phi _{L_1})^{qr}+(\phi _{L_2})^{qr}-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \root q \of {((\phi _{M_1})^{qr}+(\phi _{M_2})^{qr}-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}}~\right) ,\\ \left( \begin{array}{c}\root q \of {1-((1-\eta _{L_1}^q)^r+(1-\eta _{L_2}^q)^r-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}},\\ \root q \of {1-((1-\eta _{M_1}^q)^r+(1-\eta _{M_2}^q)^r-1)^{\frac{1}{r}}} \end{array}\right) \end{array} \right\} \\ (3)~\lambda \bigodot \daleth _{Q_1}= & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} \left( \root q \of {1-\left( \lambda (1-\phi _{L_1}^q)^r-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \root q \of {1-\left( \lambda (1-\phi _{M_1}^q)^r-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}~\right) , \\ \left( \root q \of {\left( \lambda (\eta _{L_1})^{qr}-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \root q \of {\left( \lambda (\eta _{M_1})^{qr}-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}~\right) \end{array} \right\} \\ (4)~\daleth _{Q_1}^\lambda= & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} \left( \root q \of {\left( \lambda (\phi _{L_1})^{qr}-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \root q \of {\left( \lambda (\phi _{M_1})^{qr}-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}~\right) , \\ \left( \root q \of {1-\left( \lambda (1-\eta _{L_1}^q)^r-(\lambda -1)\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}, \root q \of {1-\left( \lambda (1-\eta _{M_1}^q)^r-(\lambda -1)~\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}}\right) \end{array} \right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Schweizer-Sklar prioritized aggregation operators of qROFZNs

We have suggested a number of new aggregation operators that will be used to deal with uncertainty, priority, and inter-criteria relationships in complex MCDM applications in this section. It results in the presented multiple ordered, weighted, and prioritized aggregation operators in order to facilitate more trustworthy and adaptable decision-making structures due to the power of expressivity of qROFZNs, the flexibility of the Schweizer-Sklar t-norms and t-conorms.

q-Rung orthopair fuzzy Z numbers Schweizer-Sklar prioritized averaging aggregation operator (qROFZNPA\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{SS}$$\end{document})

This subsection presents the prioritized geometric aggregation operator, which combines the expressive strength of qROFZNs with the adaptability of the Schweizer-Sklar framework to improve decision-making under uncertainty and prioritize important criteria.

Property 3.4

Idempotency: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _Q=\daleth _{Q_i}=\{(\phi _L, \phi _M),(\eta _L, \eta _M)\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1, 2, 3, \dots \xi$$\end{document} are identical then16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} qROFZNPA_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_1},\daleth _{Q_2}, \daleth _{Q_3}, \daleth _{Q_4}\dots \daleth _{Q_\xi })=\daleth _Q \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Property 3.5

Monotonicity: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_i}=\left\{ (\phi _{L_i}, \phi _{M_i})(\eta _{L_i}, \eta _{M_i})\right\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_{i'}}=\left\{ (\phi _{L_{i'}}, \phi _{M_{i'}})(\eta _{L_{i'}}, \eta _{M_{i'}})\right\}$$\end{document} be two sets of qROFZN such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_i}\subseteq \daleth _{Q_{i'}}$$\end{document} then17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} qROFZNPA_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_1},\daleth _{Q_2}\daleth _{Q_3} \dots \daleth _{Q_\xi } )\le qROFZNPA_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_{1'}},\daleth _{Q_{2'}}\daleth _{Q_{3'}} \dots \daleth _{Q_{\xi '}} ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

Property 3.14

Idempotency:

Definition 3.17

Let qROFZN’s are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} (k)})$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Monotonicity:

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_i}=\left\{ (\phi _{L_i}, \phi _{M_i})(\eta _{L_i}, \eta _{M_i})\right\}$$\end{document} and

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_{i'}}=\left\{ (\phi _{L_{i'}}, \phi _{M_{i'}})(\eta _{L_{i'}}, \eta _{M_{i'}})\right\}$$\end{document} be two sets of qROFZN such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_i}\subseteq \daleth _{Q_{i'}}$$\end{document} then59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} qROFZNOWPG_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_1},\daleth _{Q_2}\daleth _{Q_3} \dots \daleth _{Q_\xi } )\le qROFZNOWPG_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_{1'}},\daleth _{Q_{2'}}\daleth _{Q_{3'}} \dots \daleth _{Q_{\xi '}} ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Property 3.41

Boundedness:

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_i}=\{(\phi _{L_i}, \phi _{M_i}), (\eta _{L_i}, \eta _{M_i})\}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1, 2, 3, \dots , \xi$$\end{document} be any set of qROFZN such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_{MX}}=max\daleth _{Q_i}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\daleth _{Q_{MN}}=min\daleth _{Q_i}$$\end{document} then60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \daleth _{Q_{MN}}\le qROFZNOWPG_{SS}(\daleth _{Q_1},\daleth _{Q_2}\daleth _{Q_3} \dots \daleth _{Q_\xi } )\le \daleth _{Q_{MX}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Methodological framework of qROFZNs driven MCDM

This section carry the algorithm framework to overcome the challenges for Multi-criteria decision making by using averaging and geometric aggregation operators. Here we use a numerical example for MCDM where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=\{Q_1, Q_2, Q_3, Q_4, Q_5\}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell =\{\ell _1, \ell _2, \ell _3\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\omega }=\{\widetilde{\omega }_1, \widetilde{\omega }_2, \widetilde{\omega }_3\}$$\end{document} are the alternatives, attributes and weight respectively. Where sum of the weights equal to 1 and weight is between 0 and 1. Decision-making flowchart.

Case study: electric vehicle battery performance evaluation

The evolution in electric vehicles (EVs) is a revolutionary step in global transportation, determined by the pressing need to unpredictable climate change, reduce reliance on fossil fuels, and minimize urban air pollution. By meaningfully lowering emissions, EVs, which are driven by energy generated from less-carbon or renewable sources, offer an ecological alternative to old internal combustion engine (ICE) vehicles. Beside with the environmental benefits, EVs also have economic advantages, such as economical operating costs due to the lesser cost of energy compared to fuel and low maintenance costs due to their simple and less complex designs. Developments in technology, specifically in the field of battery technology, have accelerated the adoption of EVs. Usually the selection of battery for EVs determine by range, performance and cost etc. Over the past ten years, EV batteries have become more competitive than ICE due to their significant price reduction and increased efficiency. However, as it effects the vehicle’s efficiency, user practice to use EV, and environmental control, selecting the right battery technology is critical. This case study evaluates the gap each of the five battery technologies Nickel-Cadmium (Ni-Cd), Lead-Acid, Sodium-Sulfur, Lithium-Ion, and Flow Battery-based on cycle life, safety, and charging time in order to identify which is most suitable for EV functions.

There are some specifications regarding available alternatives of batteries for EV: Nickel-Cadmium (Ni-Cd) (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_1$$\end{document}): The poor energy density of nickel-cadmium (Ni-Cd) batteries, which were first used in early EVs, restricts the vehicle’s range even though they have an acceptable cycle life and moderate charging times. Their usage of dangerous substances renders them less suitable for modern EVs and poses significant environmental and disposal problems.Lead-Acid (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_2$$\end{document}): Lead-acid batteries are a mature technology that is cheap and yet has a low energy density limiting its applicability to short and low speed vehicles. They need more time to charge and less time to run through a cycle, thus are forced to be changed more often. Their large size and harmful elements cause environmental problems and must be reprocessed and discarded with extreme caution, regardless of the truth that they are normally safe when used properly.Sodium-Sulfur (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_3$$\end{document}): Sodium-sulfur batteries are expensive to operate, a high density of energy and have an astounding cycle life, which means that they are more effective in stationary energy storage than they are in mobile electric vehicle applications. Their higher working temperatures and slower charging rates demand more complex management systems.Lithium-Ion (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_4$$\end{document}): Lithium-ion batteries are differentiated by their high energy density, which enables extended travel. They have a long cycle life, allowing for years of use before any capacity reduction is noticeable, and they charge relatively quickly for those with smartphone-class charging capabilities. Although new technologies can effectively ease these problems, safety must still be carefully handled, as it can be dangerous in extreme situations.Flow Battery (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_5$$\end{document}): The non-flammable components of Flow Batteries, an emerging technology, enable them to continue operating for a very long period of time between charges and discharges, making them extremely safe. Nevertheless, their massive bulk and low energy density, which favour stationary storage applications, prevent the majority of EVs from using them.The following attributes which are under consideration:Charging Time (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell _1}$$\end{document}): Charging time has an effect on user convenience because faster charging enables faster turnaround times for everyday use or travel, which is in line with expectations for cars that can be refuelled as rapidly as traditional cars.Cycle Life (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell _2}$$\end{document}): Cycle life, which determines battery lifespan, has an impact on total cost of ownership because long-lasting batteries are less expensive and require fewer replacements.Safety (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell _3}$$\end{document}): Electric car batteries must be safe since they must operate reliably throughout a range of driving conditions, temperature changes, and stressors without putting drivers at risk of dangerous leaks, fires, or explosions. Strong safety features are essential to reducing environmental harm from hazardous materials and averting catastrophic emergencies like thermal runaway. Table 1Benchmark values of EV battery attributes used as decision criteria.Battery TypeCharging Time (hours)Cycle Life (cycles)Safety NotesNickel-Cadmium (Ni-Cd)4–61,000–1,500Moderate risk, memory effectLead-Acid8–12300–500High risk of leakage/overheatingSodium-Sulfur (Na-S)6–8800–1,200Operates at high temperatureLithium-Ion (Li-ion)1–31,000–2,000Requires thermal managementFlow Batteries5–1010,000+Safer, scalable, but bulky

Benchmark values of EV battery attributes used as decision criteria.

The above three decision criteria are found to be critical in the process of EV battery selection based on a comprehensive literature review and a discussion with experts in the domain. The elicitation of information was done on experts of the field to get consistent assessment. The professionals gave a rating of each of the battery options relative to the criteria in terms of linguistic expressions (e.g., low, medium, high). Those linguistic assessments were then translated into qROFZN scores which included not only the extent of membership/non-membership but also the consistency of the expert ratings. The obtained qROFZN decision matrices were aggregated by use of the proposed Schweizer–Sklar based prioritized operators under the qROFZN framework.

Step 6: According to score values the arrangement of all alternative in ascending order are as following in Table 11. The compiled findings and overall rankings are shown in Table 11. It can be found that Lithium-Ion (Li-Ion) batteries continuously have the most overall performance mainly because of better energy density, cycle life, and safety. Competitiveness Sodium-Sulfur (NaS) and Flow batteries also compete in terms of sustainability and environmental impact but lag behind in terms of charging time and safety. The battery with the least rank is Lead-Acid and Ni-Cd batteries due to low energy density, short cycle life and huge environmental consideration. Step 6: According to score values the arrangement of all alternative in ascending order are as following in Table 11. The compiled findings and overall rankings are shown in Table 11. It can be found that Lithium-Ion (Li-Ion) batteries continuously have the most overall performance mainly because of better energy density, cycle life, and safety. Competitiveness Sodium-Sulfur (NaS) and Flow batteries also compete in terms of sustainability and environmental impact but lag behind in terms of charging time and safety. The battery with the least rank is Lead-Acid and Ni-Cd batteries due to low energy density, short cycle life and huge environmental consideration.

Step 6: According to score values the arrangement of all alternative in ascending order are as following in Table 11. The compiled findings and overall rankings are shown in Table 11. It can be found that Lithium-Ion (Li-Ion) batteries continuously have the most overall performance mainly because of better energy density, cycle life, and safety. Competitiveness Sodium-Sulfur (NaS) and Flow batteries also compete in terms of sustainability and environmental impact but lag behind in terms of charging time and safety. The battery with the least rank is Lead-Acid and Ni-Cd batteries due to low energy density, short cycle life and huge environmental consideration.

Ranking of alternatives by SS aggregation operators.

Therefore, the suggested qROFZN-based model proves to be able to generate a structured, transparent and reliable ranking. The results are also in agreement with industry consensus which confirms the validity of the methodology. Notably, Z-numbers and the tunable flexibility of Schweizer–Sklar aggregation guarantees stronger and more context-sensitive outcomes than traditional methods of the MCDM. Graphical representation of qROFZN\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{SS}$$\end{document} Ranking.

WASPAS approach for improved decision analysis

This section presents the WASPAS approach over qROFZNs and a numerical example over it.

The Weighted Aggregated Sum Product Assessment (WASPAS) to solve MCDM problem by combining Weighted Sum Model (WSM) and Weighted Product Model (WPM), highlighted by Zavadskas et al.38. This is a a hybrid MCDM technique to increase decision-making accuracy. It was initially created to satisfy the need for high ranking in challenging decision-making scenarios The stability, ease of use, and ability to handle a wide range of criteria under uncertainty make WASPAS a popular.

Algorithmic flow of WASPAS approach over qROFZNs

The WASPAS approach seeks a joint measure of optimality using two optimality principles. The primary optimality rule is similar to the average weighted achievement rule and the WSM technique. WSM and WPM are typical MCDM processes for evaluating multiple choices using various decision criteria. The WPM approach compares each decision alternative to others by multiplying numerous components, each of which corresponds to a decision criterion, and then multiplying these factors by the relevant criterion weights. Bridgman39 and Miller and Starr40, Ashraf et al.41 provide noteworthy examples of this strategy. Variations in results between different MCDM approaches have been noted in a variety of scenarios, making it difficult to pick the most appropriate MCDM method. Previously, researchers investigated the selection of MCDM approaches based on a variety of criteria. This research investigates the WASPAS method’s applicability and utility, finding that the value of its control parameter has a considerable influence on solution correctness and ranking performance. The study reveals that the rankings generated using the WASPAS method closely coincide with those derived by previous researchers, indicating its consistency and reliability. WASPAS Result.

Results and analysis

In the following section, we will give the results and discuss them in greater detail regarding the effectiveness of the proposed approach. To make the evaluation complete, the results are compared to the ones obtained using the classical method of WASPAS, and some of the existing methods reported in the literature. This comparative study shows the relative merits, exactness, and proficiency of our model in dealing with complicated issues of decision-making. Moreover, a sensitivity analysis is done to check the strength of the findings at different parameter configurations, which gives more insights into how the process of decision making would be stable. Lastly, the overall practical implications of the results are provided in terms of relevance of the suggested methodology to practical uses.

Comparative analysis

To demonstrate the usefulness of our strategy, the suggested algorithms are considered and evaluated in the framework of the evaluation conducted in the conditions of qROFPZNs. This comparison provide the validity, stability and effectiveness of proposed method.

As the qROFZN framework is a new construct, and has not been considered in the literature to date, we conducted a comparative analysis that we used to benchmark our proposed aggregation operations on the established WASPAS model. Even though WASPAS has been widely used on a number of fuzzy and extended structures, the use in the qROFZN environment is unexplored. In order to fill this gap, we applied both the proposed operators as well as the WASPAS methodology in the qROFZN environment. Both methods have been applied to a numerical problem and the obtained scoring and ranking results are discussed in Table 13. It is also important to mention that the difference in the scores and ranking between the two methods is not that high, still, the best alternative is the same, and it proves the consistency of the Comparison Result.

The convergence of the final ranking result attained by the proposed qROFZN\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} model and the well-known WASPAS method aids as a strong confirmation of the study’s result. This position, regardless of the primarily different mathematical foundations of the two approaches, meaningfully supports the validity and credibility of the results. The comparison between qROFZN\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} and WASPAS method shows the optimum alternative in both cases is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_4$$\end{document} show in table 13. The qROFZN\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} framework, with its enriched size to model minimize the uncertainty, imprecision and with reliability, whereas the WASPAS method, well-known for its easy calculation with accuracy in ranking, reached at a consistent solution. It means that the final ranking of alternatives is a robust and reliable image of the evaluated criteria and expert assessments, rather than the outcome of a specific mathematical model. Figure 3 illustrates comparison of the results attained by qROFZPA\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} with WASPAS.Fig. 3Graphical representation of qROFZPA\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} and WASPAS Ranking.

Graphical representation of qROFZPA\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} and WASPAS Ranking.

The convergence with the WASPAS technique gives as independent verification, proving that the qROFZN\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{ss}$$\end{document} model enhanced uncertainty management, provides reliable and authentic results.

Comparative analysis with traditional approach

We present a comparison analysis with conventional fuzzy MCDM techniques to highlight the uniqueness and benefits of the proposed method.

In order to solve real-life issues, which are ambiguous by nature, and have high levels of uncertainty, we came up with a framework that combines the theory of constraints and new solution methods endorsed by fuzzy Z-number-driven decision-making. Two new algorithms were found in this context and tested in the environment of qROFZN. In comparison with conventional methods, which in most cases can hardly handle multi-dimensional information about the hesitation and reliability of the grades, the suggested algorithms will give a total and unambiguous ranking of all the viable choices regardless of the presence of a general global optimum or not. The present techniques do not tend to manipulate the dependability of grades in two dimensional framework with broad uncertainty especially when decision details is coded in qROFZNs. Contrary to this, our techniques can take full advantage of the structural benefits of qROFZNs, allowing to place a strong treatment of the reliability and restriction measures, the levels of hesitation, and the wide variety of uncertainty that can be controlled through the parameter of q. The validity and extensibility of the research proposed approach was verified by a comparative analysis with some state-of-the-art fuzzy and fuzzy Z-number based models suggested in the literature. The results of this analysis are given in Table 15 and a characteristic based comparison is shown in Table 14.Table 14Analytical Analysis Via Existing Methodologies.MethodsRange ParameterMembership2-DReliabilityGeneralizationZadeh8NoYesNoNoNoZadeh8NoNoNoYesNoAtanassov13NoYesNoNoNoYager16NoYesYesNoNoYager24NoYesYesNoYesKumar et al.33YesYesYesNoYesProposed ApproachYesYesYesYesYes

Sensitivity analysis

Through sensitivity analysis, we test the strength of proposed method by considering varying values of parameter of the Schweizer–Sklar aggregation operator called r. Looking at various different sets of values, we can answer whether the alternative positions are highly sensitive to parameter choices or not.Table 16Ranking of options by SS’s parameter r at Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis.

Results in 16 and in graph 4 for finding results reveal that alternative Q4 achieves the highest score value with varying parameter r which is the best alternative. The variation in the absolute scores is a small response to changes in the r but the general ranking order remains the same at any value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r < 0$$\end{document}. This consistency demonstrates that the proposed model of decision-making is strong, credible and not excessively sensitive to the various values of parameters.

Practical implication

The study findings are, evidently, applicable in the process of choosing batteries in EVs. Some of such contradictory variables which decision-makers need to address to assess batteries in real-world application with ambiguous or imprecise data include energy capacity, cycle life, charging speed and safety etc. In practice, one has to select a suitable EV-battery, which is a trade-off among a range of competing variables, such as energy density, charging time, cost, security, longevity, and ecological effects. The classical methods of MCDM normally fail to capture the natural uncertainty and vagueness of expert conjecture of such parameters.

Our proposed approach provides a firmer framework as it can be used to handle more uncertainty with the help of the qROFZNs and the newly developed aggregation operators. This will allow the decision-makers - EV manufacturers and policymakers and sustainability planners to make more credible and transparent judgments. To take an example, the approach can assist manufacturers in concentrating on battery technologies that are not only high-performance oriented, but also long-term ecologically sustainable. Similarly, this model can inform policymakers in their approximate of standards of procurement or subsidy policies of EV adoption that would introduce balance between technological performance and environmental responsibility.

By setting the results within this practical context, the proposed method demonstrates the theoretical soundness of the proposed solution, as well as how the methodology can be applied to the decision-making process regarding sustainable EV battery choice.

Technical, economic and environmental trade-offs of selected alternative

The solution that has been selected as the first place is a compromise of technical, economical and environmental implications of the battery option. Technically, it is outstanding in terms of extended operating cycle, rapid charge and improved safety assurance which are decisive to manufacturers and final users. Economically, the chosen option provides a good amount of performance, how investments and operation costs can be flexible without affecting the reliability of results in large-scale operation. On the environmental front, since long live span minimizes the frequency of replacements, which becomes minimization of the demand of raw materials, waste generation is minimized as well. In addition, it reduce the use of fossil fuel which contribute to reduce the carbon emission. All these advantages make the proposed method provide the best answer in terms of trade off in technical, economic and environmental aspects.

The practical importance of the proposed framework is that it can be applied to the selection of EV battery and policy-making in the real world. The process enables a decision-maker to compare the quality of battery options based on quantitative performance measures (cost, efficiency, and longevity) and on qualitative measures like safety, sustainability, and reliability by combining q-Rung Orthopair Fuzzy Z-numbers with the Schweizer–Sklar aggregation operator. This methodology is a good way of dealing with uncertainty and expertise credibility, and provides a clearer and more data-driven foundation of the best battery technologies. It may also be easily applied in EV design assessments or policies to aid in making sustainable and dependable decision-making.

Conclusion

For real-world MCDM scenarios, it is very rare and often impossible to select an option that satisfies all of the critical requirements. To get over these challenges, the Schweizer Sklar approach is employed; it is a sophisticated mechanism that uses imprecise data to produce a compromise response. In sectors like engineering, human resources, healthcare, education, mining, auto manufacturing, and logistics, this method is frequently employed. The q-rung orhtopair is a generalized form of instuitionistic fuzzy set and pythagorean fuzzy set that encomprise more data for optimum result in MCDM problem. The credibility and truthness of data is measured by Z-number in fuzzy set theory that make MCDM problem solving more reliable. In this article, with the collaboration of q-rung orthopair fuzzy set, Z-number and aggregation operators of Schweizer Sklar method we defined series of novel prioritized aggregation operators that solves MCDM problems by efficiently handling ambiguity, conflicting criteria, ensures more reliable and fair decision-making outcomes.

In this research we introduced operations of qROFZN, elaborate the qROFZN with Schweizer Sklar method and defined Schweizer Sklar priorities aggregation operators. in this study, we provide essential theorem, lammas and properties for the authenticity of aggregation operators. To check the efficiency and validity of the proposed method here we use the case study to chose the best battery for EVs in available option. In which proposed prioritized aggregation operators based approach is applied to choose optimum alternative. By remarkable potential of qROFZNs, we have effectively made the Lithium-Ion battery the key component of Es, igniting the future with performance . Next, we used WASPAS method for comparison analysis. The results attain by proposed method gives flexible, adaptable, efficacious and more reliable approach to solved MCDM problems.

By making more dependable rankings, adapting easily to changing parameters and representing robustness in sensitivity analyses, this study presents a unique fuzzy MCDM approach that outperforms traditional models. By focusing on industry-relevant factors including battery’s cycle life, charging time, and safety, the method assurances practical implementation. It provides a reliable tool that promotes theoretical discussions and aids professionals in making knowledgeable decisions in uncertain conditions.

Limitations of the study

Although the qROFZ-based technique is favorable, it does have some drawbacks. The strategy was verified using only one case study, which is a restriction. The use of a single case study may limit the framework’s conclusions’ generalizability. The single case study was further hampered by the inability to adapt the decision framework to larger scale decision-making situations, which would now require a high level of computational complexity. A third restriction is that the assumption that decision inputs are static may not apply in real-world circumstances where inputs vary in time. Each limitation presents opportunity for future progress. Future work could include investigating the approach in different applications (e.g., renewable energy, health care sector, urban infrastructure), conducting investigations with large scale datasets, integrate it with other more powerful methods (e.g., machine learning, optimization) or generalizing the model to decision-making dynamic systems. All of these future work options would be beneficial as ways of adding value to this methodology.

Future endeavours

In an attempt to expand the boundaries and the applicability of this research, future research can concentrate on the incorporation of the proposed aggregation operators into actual real-time dynamic MCDM settings where data keep on changing. The investigation on the possibility of other case studies in areas like healthcare, smart cities, environmental engineering in support of developing the effectiveness of the model will be studied.
